>   
> The claims in this comment rely heavily on speculation and modern conspiracy thinking, not on what we actually know from ancient sources. Let’s break down the major points.  
>   
> ### **1. The Council of Nicaea did *not* outlaw interpretations of Jesus to serve the Empire**  
>   
> There is **no historical evidence** that Nicaea (AD 325) was a meeting designed to “control interpretations” or reshape how Jesus was perceived. The council dealt with **one issue only**: the Arian controversy—whether Christ was fully divine or a created being.  
>   
> * They **did not edit the New Testament canon**,  
> * They **did not rewrite biblical texts**,  
> * And they did **not silence any competing books**.  
>   
> By the time of Nicaea, the four Gospels, Paul’s letters, and the major New Testament writings were already regarded as authoritative across Christian communities for **over a century**. Church Fathers like Irenaeus (AD 180) and Origen (early 200s) quote them as a unified set long before Constantine was even born.  
>   
> If the Roman Empire intended to reshape Christianity to its political advantage, the alleged “rewriting” would have had to happen **centuries earlier**, across dozens of languages and communities that were often persecuted and scattered. That has no historical basis.  
>   
> ### **2. No evidence the Roman Empire pressured Peter, James, or John to accept Paul**  
>   
> This is an absolutely unsupported claim. The Empire at the time of Peter, James, John, and Paul was not “pro-Christian”—it was **actively persecuting Christians** and saw the movement as suspicious, illegal, and socially disruptive.  
>   
> The idea that the Empire “pressured” the apostles to accept Paul fails for several reasons:  
>   
> * **Rome had no interest in internal doctrinal disputes** among a tiny, persecuted Jewish sect.  
> * The apostles were **Jewish fishermen and tradesmen**, politically powerless, living far from Roman social centers.  
> * The Roman state didn’t adopt Christianity until nearly **300 years later** under Constantine.  
>   
> There is **zero ancient source**—Christian, Roman, or hostile—that describes Rome influencing apostolic relationships.  
> Not Tacitus.  
> Not Suetonius.  
> Not Pliny.  
> Not any early Christian writer.  
> Not archaeology.  
>   
> This claim appears nowhere until modern conspiracy circles.  
>   
> ### **3. Peter affirming Paul has nothing to do with Peter’s denial of Jesus**  
>   
> Peter’s denial happened **before** the resurrection, when he was afraid of being arrested. Using that event to claim his later judgment was unreliable is logically inconsistent.  
>   
> After the resurrection:  
>   
> * Peter becomes the **leading apostle** in Jerusalem (Acts 1–12).  
> * He publicly debates, teaches, and performs leadership roles with full confidence.  
> * He calls Paul “**our beloved brother**” and affirms Paul’s letters as **Scripture** (2 Peter 3:15–16).  
>   
> Peter’s earlier denial is actually proof of the opposite point: he **learned humility** and became extremely cautious and serious in matters of faith. To dismiss his later discernment because of a moment of fear decades earlier is simply unfair.  
>   
> ### **4. The apostles did not need “reassurance” to affirm Paul—history shows they evaluated him thoroughly**  
>   
> In the New Testament and in early church writings:  
>   
> * Paul’s teachings were **examined**, **questioned**, and ultimately **approved** by James, Peter, and John (Galatians 2).  
> * They gave him “the right hand of fellowship”—a loaded term meaning full doctrinal unity.  
> * If Paul were teaching something different about Jesus, the apostles would have rejected him, just as they rejected other false teachers in the early church.  
>   
> The idea that the apostles were confused, manipulated, or pressured contradicts the picture we have of them as **strong, persecuted, independent leaders** who repeatedly clashed with Jewish authorities, Roman rulers, and internal heretical groups. These were not easily swayed men.  
>   
> ### **5. The Roman Empire did not shape how Jesus was perceived**  
>   
> Early non-Christian Roman writers show that:  
>   
> * Christians worshiped Jesus as divine **before Constantine**,  
> * Christian theology developed **within Jewish monotheistic frameworks**,  
> * Rome often disliked Christian teachings, not shaped them.  
>   
> If Rome had tried to “make Jesus more acceptable,” they would have minimized Christian teachings that offended Roman religion—such as rejecting pagan gods and emperor worship. But Christians *never* softened those positions to please Rome.  
>   
> This shows the Empire was **not steering doctrine**—Christians openly resisted Roman expectations for centuries.  
>   
> ---  
>   
> ### **Conclusion**  
>   
> The narrative that “Rome changed Jesus and forced the apostles to accept Paul” has no basis in ancient history, archaeology, or textual scholarship.  
>   
> * The Bible wasn’t rewritten at Nicaea.  
> * Rome did not control apostolic doctrine.  
> * Peter’s earlier denial does not undermine his later affirmation of Paul.  
> * The early Christians’ beliefs about Jesus were established **long before imperial involvement**.  
>   
> This view is shaped by modern myth, not historical evidence

> ​@pokolotheii  
> You bring up several valid historical points—especially about the disputed books, the geographic diversity of early Christianity, and the fact that Nicaea did not define the biblical canon. But your conclusion still overextends what the evidence actually shows. Let’s take it point by point.  
>   
> ---  
>   
> ## **1. Condemning Arianism is *not* the same as Rome reshaping how Jesus was perceived**  
>   
> It’s true that Nicaea condemned Arianism and required bishops to sign the creed. No disagreement there. But the key point is this:  
>   
> **Arianism was already a minority, controversial view before Nicaea.**  
>   
> * Origen, Dionysius of Alexandria, and Tertullian all wrote *against* subordinationist or Arian-like Christology long before Constantine.  
> * Irenaeus (AD 180) condemns views that treat Christ as a created being.  
> * The majority of bishops at Nicaea were already *anti-Arian* before Constantine invited them.  
>   
> The Council’s decision did not *introduce* a new interpretation—it *ratified* the overwhelmingly dominant one already held in most Christian communities for nearly two centuries.  
>   
> **Rejecting a doctrinal position ≠ reshaping Jesus to suit Rome.**  
> If anything, the Nicene position was *less* politically convenient for Rome, because it insisted Jesus was divine in a way that clashed with Roman polytheism and emperor worship.  
>   
> ---  
>   
> ## **2. “Restricting interpretation” is not the same as “changing perception”**  
>   
> Every religious community—Jewish, Christian, Muslim, Buddhist—eventually draws boundaries on interpretation. That’s simply how orthodoxy works.  
>   
> What Nicaea did *not* do:  
>   
> * introduce a new image of Jesus,  
> * suppress widespread alternative portrayals of Jesus,  
> * reinterpret Scripture in a novel or imperial direction.  
>   
> What it *did* do:  
>   
> * clarify how Christians had already been describing Jesus for generations.  
>   
> So yes, Nicaea “restricted” Arian exegesis.  
> But no, this does not imply that Rome shaped Christian doctrine to its advantage. The historical data shows the opposite: Rome repeatedly clashed with Christians **because** they insisted on Christ’s divinity and refused to accommodate Roman religion.  
>   
> ---  
>   
> ## **3. The disputed canon books do not support the idea of imperial influence**  
>   
> You’re correct that:  
>   
> * Revelation was questioned in the East.  
> * Hebrews, James, Jude, and 2 Peter were questioned in the West.  
> * Texts like Hermas, Barnabas, and Didache were widely read.  
>   
> But this diversity actually *undermines* the idea of Roman shaping of doctrine.  
>   
> Here’s why:  
>   
> * The divergences reflect **natural regional differences**, not Roman policy.  
> * No emperor was involved in choosing Scripture until centuries *after* these debates began.  
> * The evidence shows **grassroots, community-driven discernment**, not top-down imperial pressure.  
>   
> If the Empire were shaping doctrine, we would expect:  
>   
> * uniform acceptance of the same books everywhere,  
> * politically safe or pro-Roman texts becoming canonical,  
> * suppressed writings disappearing.  
>   
> Yet we see the exact opposite: messy, organic, decentralized debates.  
>   
> ---  
>   
> ## **4. Political pressures shaped Christian courage, not Christian theology**  
>   
> You argue that Roman persecution and internal tensions “shaped the early church indirectly.” But there’s a difference between shaping **behavior under persecution** and shaping **doctrine**.  
>   
> Historical evidence shows:  
>   
> * Roman pressure forced Christians to gather discreetly  
>   —but did **not** shape their beliefs about Jesus.  
> * Jewish–Gentile tensions shaped community life  
>   —but did **not** reshape Christology.  
> * Christians appeared suspicious to Rome  
>   —but Rome did not care about internal Christian debates until the 4th century.  
>   
> If anything, persecution *strengthened* theological stubbornness.  
> A movement willing to die rather than pinch incense to Caesar was not easily reshaped by political pressure.  
>   
> ---  
>   
> ## **5. “Followers of the Way” does not show doctrinal evolution—only naming evolution**  
>   
> You’re absolutely right that early Christians used the term “the Way.” But names shifting over time is normal in movements:  
>   
> * Jews were called “Hebrews,” “Israelites,” “the People,” etc., at different times.  
> * Buddhists weren’t originally called “Buddhists.”  
> * Pharisees and Sadducees didn’t start with those labels either.  
>   
> A change in terminology is not evidence of a change in the core message or view of Jesus.  
>   
> ---  
>   
> ## **6. The key point remains: nothing in the record shows Rome influencing apostolic decisions**  
>   
> Your revised claim is more modest, but the core issue is still this:  
>   
> **There is no historical evidence—none—that Rome indirectly shaped how Peter, James, or John evaluated Paul, or shaped early Christology.**  
>   
> The apostles lived and died:  
>   
> * under local Jewish and Roman hostility,  
> * decades before any imperial favoring of Christianity,  
> * with no record of Roman involvement in their disputes.  
>   
> Even the earliest Roman references to Christianity (Tacitus, Suetonius, Pliny) show *persecution*, not influence.  
>   
> ---  
>   
> ## **Conclusion**  
>   
> You’ve shifted your argument to a more historically grounded position, but the conclusion still overreaches:  
>   
> * **Condemning Arianism** does not imply Rome reshaped Jesus.  
> * **Disputed books** reflect internal Christian debates, not imperial direction.  
> * **Persecution and political tension** shaped Christian courage, not Christian doctrine.  
> * **Naming shifts** do not imply theological shifts.  
> * And **no ancient source** connects Rome to apostolic decisions or early Christology.  
>   
> We can acknowledge complexities without implying influences for which there is simply no evidence.

> ​@NiToNi2002  Your comment is simply out of step with mainstream historical scholarship. The consensus among contemporary historians, including non-Christian and atheist scholars is that Jesus of Nazareth was a real historical figure. Claims of “zero hard evidence” are rejected across the field; Jesus is attested not only in early Christian sources but also in multiple independent non-Christian references (Roman and Jewish), which is more than we have for many ancient figures no one doubts.  
>   
>  Your claim that the Gospels were written “when no eyewitnesses would still have been around” is also false. A 40–60 year gap is well within living memory in the ancient world, especially in oral cultures trained to preserve testimony. Early Christian tradition consistently ties the Gospels to eyewitnesses or close companions of eyewitnesses, and the texts themselves explicitly appeal to living witnesses, which would be reckless if none existed to correct falsehoods.  
>   
>  Demanding modern-style first-hand documentation misunderstands ancient historiography. Virtually all ancient history relies on mediated testimony, not personal diaries. By those standards, the New Testament is unusually early, well-attested, and grounded in named individuals, places, and events.  
>   
>  Finally, dismissing Christians as indifferent to facts or misrepresenting Jesus’ teachings as merely “anti-capitalist” rhetoric avoids the actual historical question. One can disagree with Christian theology or ethics, but denying Jesus’ existence or the eyewitness foundations of the New Testament requires rejecting the same historical methods used for almost all of antiquity.  

> **@frobsonsdesenhos**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kuMhSFXnr5I&lc=Ugwkr9g4zCVccBrPvql4AaABAg.AOIp6AStrgHAT4vDEARGTQ)  
>
> ​@mdot2495 The Columbus comparison just doesn’t work, it’s a category error. Columbus’s “discovery” claim is a later national myth built centuries after the fact and easily corrected once broader historical data (Vikings, indigenous presence) was considered. Paul, by contrast, is evaluated through contemporary, internal sources from the same movement and time period.  
>   
> We have multiple early witnesses who knew Paul personally and had every incentive to correct him if he were a “wolf”: Peter explicitly affirms Paul’s teachings (2 Peter 3:15–16), Luke records Paul’s ministry alongside the apostles, and John who outlived Paul never rebukes or distances the church from him. Even Paul’s critics are acknowledged within the texts, which actually strengthens their credibility rather than undermining it.  
>   
> So this isn’t a case of later generations mythologizing a hero; it’s early, cross-checked testimony from allies and potential rivals who were still alive to object. Skepticism is fine, but comparing Paul to Columbus ignores how historical evidence is actually weighed.  

> **@frobsonsdesenhos**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kuMhSFXnr5I&lc=Ugwkr9g4zCVccBrPvql4AaABAg.AOIp6AStrgHAT4vvKRrf5R)  
>
> ​@joyboy-u5m This response mixes a real emotional question with a few false assumptions, so it helps to separate them.  
>   
> First, the world being broken is not an argument against Paul or Christianity, it’s actually one of their core claims. Christianity never teaches that the world would instantly become a utopia; it teaches that human freedom, sin, and power-seeking persist, and that those who genuinely live by love and compassion are often killed precisely because they challenge unjust systems. That’s not evidence the message is false; it’s evidence it’s costly. Jesus himself says this explicitly.  
>   
> Second, dismissing all historical evidence because “it can be twisted” is self-defeating. If we take that route, then no claim about the past good or bad can be meaningfully evaluated. History doesn’t work by vibes or suspicion alone; it works by early sources, multiple witnesses, internal consistency, and hostile verification. By those standards, Paul is unusually well-attested and closely scrutinized by people who knew him personally.  
>   
> Third, accusing Paul by association (“powerful people are pedos, therefore Paul could be”) isn’t skepticism, it’s speculation. Paul wasn’t powerful, wealthy, protected, or celebrated in his lifetime. He was imprisoned, beaten, executed, and constantly opposed. There is zero historical evidence, early or late, of moral scandal attached to him, which is remarkable given how controversial he was. If something like that existed, his enemies would have used it immediately.  
>   
> Finally, God doesn’t “force” anyone through Paul’s teaching. Paul himself insists on free response, conscience, patience, and love, coercion is explicitly rejected. The Christian claim is not that God overrides freedom, but that He invites, persuades, and waits. The fact that many reject the message is already built into the theology.  
>   
> So the question of suffering is serious—but it doesn’t undermine Paul’s credibility, nor does suspicion without evidence replace careful historical reasoning.  

> **@frobsonsdesenhos**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kuMhSFXnr5I&lc=Ugwkr9g4zCVccBrPvql4AaABAg.AOIp6AStrgHAT4wFLBSVHt)  
>
> ​@dennisteunissen8014 Your claim rests on misunderstandings of both Jesus and Paul.  
>   
> First, Paul didn’t overturn Jesus on food laws, Jesus himself declares all foods clean (Mark 7:18–19), and the Jerusalem Council (led by Peter and James, not Paul alone) explicitly ruled that Gentile believers were not bound to circumcision or Mosaic dietary laws (Acts 15). Paul is applying that shared apostolic decision, not freelancing against Jesus.  
>   
> Second, circumcision was never treated by Jesus as a moral command but as a covenant sign for Israel. Paul’s argument is that righteousness comes from faith, not ethnic markers, again echoing Jesus’ own focus on the heart over external rituals.  
>   
> Third, the “false prophecy” charge is vague. Paul makes pastoral expectations and conditional hopes, not date-setting predictions. The New Testament distinguishes clearly between prophecy and human plans, and nowhere is Paul condemned by the early church as a false prophet, despite intense scrutiny from both critics and fellow apostles.  
>   
> Paul isn’t contradicting Jesus; he’s consistently applying Jesus’ teachings to a new, Gentile context, with the public approval of the other apostles.

> ​@EliotDunsmore your comment mixes up a lot of history.  
>   
> First, the books of the New Testament were not “approved” or created at the First Council of Nicaea. Nicaea dealt with the divinity of Christ in response to Arianism, it did not decide the canon. The four Gospels were already widely recognized and used across Christian communities long before the 4th century because of their apostolic origin and continuous liturgical use.  
>   
> Second, the so-called “apocalyptic” or Gnostic texts like the Gospel of Thomas were written in the mid-to-late 2nd century, long after the apostles had died. They lack apostolic succession, contradict earlier Christian teaching, and reflect Gnostic philosophy, not 1st-century Jewish Christianity. There is no evidence Jesus taught that “we are all God.” That idea comes from later Gnostic metaphysics, not from the historical Jesus grounded in Jewish monotheism.  
>   
> Third, Paul did not invent Jesus’ divinity. Before Paul even converted, Christians were already worshiping Jesus. Early creeds embedded in Paul’s letters, such as Philippians 2:6–11 show that high Christology predates him. Paul inherited that belief; he didn’t create it.  
>   
> Finally, Paul never taught that humans are “inherently evil” in a metaphysical sense. He taught that humanity is fallen and enslaved to sin, damaged, not ontologically evil. That’s very different from saying human nature itself is evil. His theology is about redemption and restoration, not about declaring humanity worthless.  
>   
> So historically and theologically, the claim doesn’t hold up.

I follow the teachings of Jesus, and I think he made them very clear, like to have compassion on others, like the woman who was about to be stoned to death for adultery and the prodigal son, where the father invites his son back to his house with unconditional love. How does this teaching of profound love speak of a desire for justice over love, and where in the Old Testament does this vengeful God actually demonstrate unconditional love like Jesus taught? Yes, I know that he fulfilled the prophecies of the Old Testament, but his nature is nothing like the God of the Old Testament, and even today, there is still violence between other religions that is used to justify a God of justice, not unconditional love. It's fine if you believe that the God of the Old Testament is the true source behind all creation, but let me ask you a question: if God is all perfect and all-loving, then how is it that we imperfect humans seem to have more compassion at times than the God of the Old Testament? Perfect example, look at the story of Abraham, where he tells God not to destroy the entire city of Sodom and Gomorrah for the sake of some righteous people or how about when Moses told God not to destroy the Israelites for building the golden calf, because of God's jealousy, and then he repented of the evil that he had in his heart. Wait a minute, I didn't know God constantly changes his mind. This does not make sense, and my conclusion is that either we have more compassion for humanity than God does, or the God of the Old Testament is not really the source behind all creation, but an intelligent and maleficent being deceiving us that he is the omnipotent God to keep us under control. How about the innocent children with their mothers who were slain by the Israelites led by Moses in the name of God? How about the story in the book of Exodus where the angel of death purges all the firstborn of Egypt, and all cattle, for the disobedience of the Pharaoh to Moses. I'm sure a majority of those firstborns were either still children or infants. So now we have to accept that the God of the Old Testament is a murderer of children, and we somehow need to justify that because he is God. Or how about the story in Samuel where God punishes Saul for not destroying all the sheep, cattle, and the king in a Canaanite city? So basically, this God desires destruction and genocide not only of sinners but also of innocent children, infants, and cattle, because he delights in seeing complete desolation. And if you really read the Old Testament carefully, why does God appear to the Israelites in the shape of a cloud during the day, and a pillar of fire at night? Not to mention, how about the vision of Ezekiel, where he saw a flying craft with even the description of seeing wheels of Topaz with a head full of eyes intersecting one another, and the beings that he saw, which seemed to resemble humanoid-like creatures. If anything, these beings sound like extraterrestrials with advanced technology that to ancient people back in those days, would appear as gods to them, but now we know better, and even the government is admitting that other extraterrestrials have been in contact with us for so long. And if you really read the New Testament carefully, you'll remember that Jesus referred to the father of the Pharisees as the devil, who was a liar and a thief in the beginning, and the Pharisees were very devout to their God, Yahweh. Or how about when Jesus told his disciples, If a child asks for bread, what kind of father would give him a stone, and if a child asks for a fish, what kind of father would give him a serpent? Sound familiar? In the Old Testament, when the Israelites asked Moses for water, Yahweh told Moses to strike a rock, for water to gush out. Remember when the Israelites asked Moses to tell Yahweh to send them more food because they were sick of eating Manna? And what did Yahweh send to the Israelites who were complaining? Serpents that poisoned them, so what kind of loving father would abuse his children, all because they want more food? I'm sorry, but the God of the Old Testament is not a God of love but a God of vengeance, and I refuse to worship such a vengeful God that requires blood sacrifices for forgiveness.

> ​@closingtheloop2593   
> The idea that Paul “changed” Jesus’ message is not supported by the New Testament or early Christian history. Paul’s writings consistently expand on Jesus’ teachings, not replace them. When Paul emphasizes faith, he is echoing Jesus’ own message that genuine faith produces transformed behavior—“a tree is known by its fruit.” Paul repeatedly teaches the same principle: salvation is by God’s grace, but true faith necessarily results in obedience, love, and moral renewal (e.g., Romans 6, Galatians 5). This is not a contradiction; it is the same message expressed in theological terms for a broader Gentile audience.  
>   
> Furthermore, the claim that Paul was somehow at odds with the original apostles ignores the very sources that describe their relationship. Paul’s apostleship was explicitly affirmed by Peter, James, and John (Galatians 2:7–9), and Peter himself refers to Paul’s writings as authoritative (2 Peter 3:15–16). The early church—those closest to the events—universally recognized Paul as a legitimate apostle and missionary. There is no historical evidence of a doctrinal conflict between Paul and the Jerusalem apostles; the only disputes recorded were about practical issues such as the admission of Gentiles, and those were resolved in unity at the Council of Jerusalem (Acts 15).  
>   
> The accusation that Jesus warned against someone “like Paul” is also misplaced. Jesus’ warning about deceivers appearing “in deserts” or “inner rooms” was about false messiahs claiming to be Christ, not apostles preaching Christ (Matthew 24:23–26). Paul never claimed to be the Messiah—he proclaimed Jesus as Lord and himself as a servant. Reading Jesus’ warnings as predictions about Paul is an interpretation without textual or historical basis.  
>   
> In short, Paul’s message is deeply continuous with Jesus’ own teaching, the apostles endorsed him, and the early church preserved a picture of harmony—not conflict—between Paul and the original disciples.  

> **@frobsonsdesenhos**

>
> ​@closingtheloop2593   
> The idea that Paul distorted Jesus’ message simply doesn’t hold up to the historical or textual evidence. Paul doesn’t replace Jesus’ teachings with “easy faith”; he explains the same message in theological terms for Gentile audiences. Jesus taught that authentic faith produces obedience, love, repentance, and transformation—Paul teaches exactly the same thing (Romans 6; Galatians 5; Ephesians 2:8–10). Paul is expanding Jesus’ message for new contexts, not reinventing it.  
>   
> You also introduces ideas Jesus never taught. There is no evidence in the Gospels that Jesus’ core message was “finding God in ourselves” or some kind of inner self-realization ethic. Jesus preached the arrival of God’s kingdom, repentance, faith in him as Messiah, and obedience to God’s will. While Jesus absolutely taught moral transformation, he never framed it as discovering divinity or goodness within oneself. That notion is foreign to the Jewish context and absent from the texts.  
>   
> The claim that Peter “can easily be viewed as a false teacher” is also historically baseless. In the actual early church, Peter is universally recognized as a foundational witness—*the* apostle commissioned by Jesus to shepherd the flock (John 21:15–17). Calling Peter a false teacher directly contradicts both the New Testament and the unanimous testimony of the earliest Christian communities. It also severely undermines your own credibility: if Peter, the chief apostle who walked with Jesus, is dismissed, then no claim about “what Jesus really meant” can stand on any historical grounding. You're essentially rejecting the very sources that preserve Jesus’ teachings while still trying to appeal to those teachings.  
>   
> Finally, the idea that Jesus warned specifically against someone like Paul is a misreading. Jesus’ warnings about deceivers in “deserts” or “inner rooms” (Matthew 24:23–26) refer to *false messiahs* claiming to be Christ, not apostles preaching Christ. Paul never claimed messiahship—only to proclaim the risen Jesus. Moreover, Paul’s apostleship was explicitly affirmed by Peter, James, and John (Galatians 2:7–9), and Peter refers to Paul’s letters as authoritative (2 Peter 3:15–16). There is no historical evidence of doctrinal conflict between them.  
>   
> In short, Jesus didn’t preach inner-divinity, Peter wasn’t a false teacher, and Paul didn’t contradict Jesus or the apostles. Attacking Peter removes the very foundation the commenter relies on to interpret Jesus—while the actual historical record shows unity, not division, in the early church.

> ​@Mplsfitter539Matthew 10:5–6 (“Do not go among the Gentiles… go rather to the lost sheep of the house of Israel”) is usually explained by theologians in terms of **salvation history, mission strategy, and audience**, not as Jesus rejecting Gentiles. Here are the main theological explanations:  
>   
> ---  
>   
> ### 1. **Priority, not exclusion**  
>   
> Most theologians argue Jesus is setting a **temporary priority**, not an eternal limitation.  
>   
> * Israel was God’s **covenant people**, already awaiting the Messiah.  
> * The gospel is presented **“to the Jew first”** (cf. Romans 1:16).  
> * This does *not* mean Gentiles are unimportant, only that Israel is addressed first.  
>   
> This fits with Jesus’ own later actions (healing Gentiles, praising Gentile faith).  
>   
> ---  
>   
> ### 2. **A specific, limited mission**  
>   
> Matthew 10 describes a **particular mission** given to the Twelve at that moment in Jesus’ ministry.  
>   
> * It is **not the final or universal mission**.  
> * After the resurrection, Jesus explicitly expands the mission to **all nations** (Matthew 28:18–20).  
>   
> Many theologians point out that interpreting Matthew 10 as permanent would contradict Jesus’ own later commands.  
>   
> ---  
>   
> ### 3. **Messianic context**  
>   
> Jesus is presenting himself as **Israel’s Messiah**, fulfilling Old Testament promises.  
>   
> * The phrase “lost sheep of Israel” echoes prophetic language (Ezekiel 34; Jeremiah 50).  
> * Israel must first be confronted with the fulfillment of their own Scriptures.  
>   
> If Israel accepts or rejects the Messiah, that response becomes the turning point for the wider mission.  
>   
> ---  
>   
> ### 4. **Practical and pedagogical reasons**  
>   
> Some scholars note practical considerations:  
>   
> * The disciples were Jewish and culturally unprepared for cross-cultural mission at that stage.  
> * Beginning within Israel allowed them to learn proclamation, healing, and discipleship **before** engaging the wider world.  
>   
> Acts reflects this same progression: Jerusalem → Judea → Samaria → Gentiles.  
>   
> ---  
>   
> ### 5. **Matthew’s broader theology**  
>   
> Matthew’s Gospel itself undercuts any idea of ethnic exclusivism:  
>   
> * Gentile women appear in Jesus’ genealogy (Matthew 1).  
> * Gentiles show exemplary faith (the Magi, the centurion).  
> * Jesus heals and praises Gentiles.  
> * The Gospel ends with a universal commission.  
>   
> So Matthew 10:5–6 must be read within that **larger narrative arc**.  
>   
> ---  
>   
> ### In short  
>   
> Theologians generally agree Matthew 10:5–6 teaches:  
>   
> * **Mission order**, not ethnic favoritism  
> * **A temporary instruction**, not a permanent boundary  
> * **Fulfillment of Israel’s role**, leading to a global mission  
>   
> It’s about *how* God unfolds salvation in history, not about *who* God ultimately cares for.

> @frobsonsdesenhosthe point is exactly that we believe that is the “truth” because it is all we have left to read about these people and who they may or may not have actually been like. These guys aren’t trying to tell you your beliefs are a lie, my friend—they bring up points of fascination to make you think about biblical scripture in less black and white terms. Do you not find it counter-intuitive that Jesus, THE descendent of heaven, was for some reason regularly defined as John the Baptist? They didn’t (from what I can tell) refer to Jesus literally as John reincarnate, but as the embodiment of the faith that he lived. If Jesus is THE guy, there’s something not explained in terms of sociocultural dynamics between them. John was loved, yes, but that misses the dynamic we’re feeling but not seeing in the text. To seemingly take the most holy man below God and, on top of that, to imply that even he bends the knee as an extension of Johns will is fascinating. To be clear, I don’t argue this is REALLY, exactly what happened; rather that it’s interesting and for me, personally fulfilling to consider the ramifications of such interplay in a religious text, as well as its mythos beyond the words on a page.

Was Christianity really founded by Jesus — or by Paul?
In this lecture, we explore one of history’s most controversial questions: who truly built the religion that would come to dominate the Western world?
Paul of Tarsus — a wealthy, educated, Hellenized Jew — didn’t just spread the message of Jesus. He redefined it.
From reshaping salvation into belief rather than experience, to building the organizational structure of the Church, Paul transformed a small spiritual movement into a global empire of faith.
But was he truly divinely inspired… or a strategic agent of Rome?
This lecture traces Paul’s mysterious life — his sudden conversion, his Roman protection, and his obsession with church hierarchy — to uncover a deeper political and psychological story.
By the end, you’ll see why many historians believe Paul was not only the first Christian missionary — he was the architect of Christianity itself.

Paul didn't mention Yoshua ben Yosef or quote him, as Paul believed the true Christ, the Anointed One, was none other than Gaius Julius Caesar, or as he became, Divus Iulius. His life, his triumphal entry into Rome, his sacrifice at the hands of the conspirators, and his subsequent deification laid the very foundation for the "Christ event." The narratives surrounding Jesus of Nazareth, including figures like Judas the Galilean, were later literary constructs, assembled by the likes of Mark, to translate Caesar's profound impact and divine status into a more accessible, localized, and ritualistic framework for a broader, less Romanized audience. Paul, in his unparalleled zeal and profound understanding, truly saw the cosmic implications of Caesar's life and death, recognizing in him the fulfillment of divine promise and the pattern for all who would be "in Christ."

>
> ⁠@IndieBaptist  
>   
>   
> Paul of Tarsus: Friend or Foe of the Gospel of Jesus?  
>   
> Clever Corrupter or Honest Visionary?  
>   
> 🔹 The Question  
>   
> Was Paul a friend to the true teachings of Jesus,  
> or a foe, whose clever theology corrupted the pure Gospel of the Kingdom?  
>   
> Was he a brilliant missionary,  
> or an architect of confusion, blending the life of Jesus with blood doctrines, old systems, and religious control?  
>   
> This question deserves light, not labels. So let’s walk it through.  
>   
> ⸻  
>   
> 🔹 What Did Jesus Teach?  
>   
> Jesus preached:  
>    •   The Kingdom of God is within you.  
>    •   God is your Father.  
>    •   You are brothers and sisters.  
>    •   Love is the greatest law.  
>    •   Righteousness is relational, not ritual.  
>    •   And salvation is found in the living relationship between the soul and the Father—not in sacrifices, blood, or law.  
>   
> He never taught original sin.  
> He never demanded belief in His death.  
> He invited people to follow His life.  
>   
> ⸻  
>   
> 🔹 What Did Paul Teach?  
>   
> Paul never met Jesus in the flesh.  
> His conversion came through a spiritual vision, which may have been real—but it wasn’t firsthand discipleship.  
>   
> Paul’s writings:  
>    •   Introduced atonement theology—that Jesus died for our sins.  
>    •   Reframed salvation as belief in the resurrection and lordship of Christ, often detached from His actual teachings.  
>    •   Brought in terms like “justification by faith,” “original sin,” and “the curse of the law”—ideas foreign to Jesus’ actual gospel.  
>    •   Used Roman, Greek, and Jewish logic to frame a hybrid theology that spread fast… but often left behind the living Jesus.  
>   
> ⸻  
>   
> 🔹 Was He a Foe?  
>   
> If we define “foe” as someone who intentionally corrupted the teachings of Jesus—then no, Paul may not have been a conscious enemy.  
>   
> But if we define it as someone whose words led millions away from the simple, living Gospel of Jesus into a more abstract, blood-based belief system—then yes, Paul became a theological detour.  
>   
> He helped create the religion about Jesus,  
> while the religion with Jesus got buried.  
>   
> ⸻  
>   
> 🔹 Was He a Friend?  
>   
> If we define “friend” as someone who tried to serve Jesus with sincerity—then yes, Paul likely was trying.  
>   
> He used what he knew—Greek rhetoric, Roman logic, Jewish scriptures—to reach people in his era. He was passionate, bold, tireless.  
>   
> But passion doesn’t equal purity.  
>   
> He may have believed in Jesus without truly understanding Him.  
>   
> ⸻  
>   
> 🔹 The Verdict: Visionary or Corrupter?  
>   
> Paul was a complex vessel.  
>   
> He was a visionary of adaptation,  
> but he also laid foundations that institutional religion twisted into chains.  
>   
> He served a cosmic Christ,  
> but often missed the human Jesus.  
>   
> ⸻  
>   
> 🔹 Final Word: Let Jesus Be the Standard  
>   
> In this new age of spiritual rebirth,  
> we must return to the Jesus who lived, not just the Jesus who died.  
> We must test every apostle, preacher, and prophet by one thing:  
>   
> Does their message match the life, love, and light of Jesus the Son of Man?  
>   
> Paul’s letters may have moments of brilliance.  
> But Jesus alone is the way, the truth, and the life.

It seems unlikely that Paul was a spy for the Roman Empire. There is no credible historical or archaeological evidence supporting the claim that Paul was a Roman spy. ### **@jeffrobertson4097** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kuMhSFXnr5I&lc=UgzRRU9ZlPGMW9WibNF4AaABAg)

The problem that most people make is audience relevance and not recognizing the 2 ages/covenants (old/new), and the transition period from AD30 (death of Jesus) and AD70 (temple destruction; final end of old age/covenant). That 40 year period mimics the 40 year period from the release of bondage to Egypt/Sin and the entrance into the promise land/new covenant. 
Jesus and Paul weren’t teaching different ways into the kingdom. Jesus was speaking to Jews under the Law and correcting their expectations, saying the kingdom wasn’t political or future but already present in Him — “in your midst.” Paul was writing later to explain how people enter that kingdom: not through the Law or works, but through faith in Christ. Jesus announced the kingdom and called for repentance and allegiance; Paul explained access to it after the cross. Same message, different audiences, different problems being addressed.

Jesus spoke to Jews still under the Law before the cross, calling for covenant faithfulness and allegiance to Him as Israel approached judgment. Paul wrote after the cross, explaining that righteousness now comes through faith in Christ alone, not through the works or identity markers of the Law. Jesus exposes empty profession and disloyalty; Paul rejects law-based righteousness. Same gospel, different audiences, same first-century horizon.

> Simon Peter answered, “You are the Messiah, the Son of the living God.” 17 And Jesus answered him, “Blessed are you, Simon son of Jonah! For flesh and blood has not revealed this to you but my Father in heaven. 18 And I tell you, you are Peter, and on this rock I will build my church, and the gates of Hades will not prevail against it.   
>   
> Peter is the disciple that identifies Jesus as the Christ. The rock that Jesus is building his church on is the belief that Jesus is God's anointed one, which in this passage is embodied by the "rock" of Peter (a synchronistic play on words like @alexg7836 highlighted). This belief is critical to the foundation of the church because if you have faith that a humble and homeless carpenter turned preacher who is subjected to perfect betrayal (by his closest friends), perfect rejection (by his own people that he came to save), and perfect suffering (at the hands of an occupying empire) is the perfect expression of God's will on earth, then you can live the life of peace, love and mercy that Jesus preached because you will no longer associate the "fleshly" trappings of this world as markers of divinity or success. Jesus came to break the default mode network of humanity that correlates perceived power and honor with what is good or part of God's will.   
>   
> The life of Christ is supposed to be a perfect example of submission to the will of God the Father("not my will, but yours be done", "if you've seen me, you've seen the father"), even to the point of suffering and death. This rejection of our natural way of operating is pretty clearly displayed in the temptation story where Jesus rejects the obvious "right" way to achieve his goals and to use his authority/power. In this way you are freed to live a proper life in submission to God's will (i.e. loving God and others) without worrying about whether or not you're going to be taken advantage, betrayed, or need to defend yourself. It is a correction of humanities original fall in the garden that led to suffering in the first place (i.e. our ability to determine for ourselves what is good and bad based on what is "good for food, pleasing to the eye, and for gaining knowledge"). Then through the resurrection it is revealed that we are not saved from death by being protected, but that we are saved through death because it is not the end of us.   
>   
> At least that's my understanding.

### **@Him__Downstairs** | 👍 14 likes

A few key historical clarifications…

Hi Jiang, I really appreciate your curiosity and willingness to explore connections between historical texts and Christian scripture. Your enthusiasm is infectious. That said, I wanted to clarify a few points that might be misleading for viewers who are trying to understand the historical record:

Paul and Socrates/Caesar: The idea that Paul added these stories to the New Testament is not supported by mainstream scholarship. Paul’s letters predate the gospels and never reference Socrates or Caesar directly.

Paul mirroring Jesus: While there are literary parallels, Paul’s life as documented in Acts and his letters shows different historical circumstances. Many of the “mirroring” claims rely on creative interpretation rather than solid evidence.

Ebionites and Islam: The early Jewish-Christian sects like the Ebionites lived hundreds of years before Islam emerged. They did not directly create or influence Islam personally. That said, Islam did emerge in a region where Christianity and Judaism were present, so the Quran incorporates figures like Jesus strategically, reflecting regional religious traditions.

Historical liberties: Some of your lectures fill in gaps with imaginative reconstructions. These are interesting and thought provoking, but it’s important to clearly distinguish speculation from established historical evidence.

Your lectures are full of fascinating ideas and patterns, and they can spark curiosity, but it’s crucial for viewers to separate speculation from what historians can confidently assert.

Thanks for your work. I enjoy the discussions you start, even when I see things differently!

I don't agree with Professor that Jesus was a Gnostic. I think this was theory developed by group of his followers to make a coherent religion. I am more drawn to theory of dr Bart Erhrman that Jesus was an apocalyptic prophet and genuinely believed that he will go to Jerusalem and establish Kingdom of God on Earth. Instead he was crucified by the Romans. Which makes sense if he was all like "yeah I'm making new kingdom". The way I see early Christianity then is like: followers of Jesus were crushed. They lost their friend and teacher. Distressed mind can play many tricks, so they started seeing Jesus alive but in spiritual experience. He might've still be in his grave. Paul saw Jesus couple of years later when he was supposed to have ascended to heavens and it seems to have been same experience as direct followers had. They've become reinvigorated and started preaching Jesus. They believed he was alive (in soul not body) and his day will come IN THEIR LIFETIMES. It is evident in writings of Paul also, the earliest christian texts. Notice that Gospels weren't written yet, this may indicate that Christians didn't think long term because there was no need. After the letters of Paul we get very interesting early christian writing. Letter of Clemens, bishop of Rome. In it, he explicitly states that deaths of Peter and Paul were result of internal conflict. They did quarrel before but what would make the conflict so severe that they both died? Jewish Rebellion possibly. Imo Peter saw rebellion as second coming of Jesus, while Paul was skeptical and rather pro-Roman. This is indicated by Paul being apostle of the Gentiles and Peter of the Jews and by triumph of Pauline Christianity. Could've been other way around too, we simply have no evidence for any of that. As we know, Jewish rebellion was put down and Jerusalem destroyed and Jewish church most likely too. Pro pagan party was triumphant and Christianity was transformed. Jesus was now resurrected in flesh and ascended and won't come back soon but sometime in the future. Gospels were written and the rest of New Testament, and we got religion we have today.

Jesus being apocalyptic prophet, apostles believing they will live to second coming, resurrecting being only spiritual and deadly conflict between Peter and Paul are theories by scholars. Conflict being about Jewish rebellion and the reason New Testament and Christianity is like this is my theory (I am not an academic and it relies too much on speculation. Just a plausible scenario with too little evidence).

### **@TerryGrier**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kuMhSFXnr5I&lc=UgzbnEPUYShwBA9fuhl4AaABAg)

reminder:  

While scholars often debate exact years, here is the generally accepted sequence based on historical context, internal clues, and linguistic styles.

---

## 1. The Early Letters (c. 50 – 60 AD)

The earliest writings of the New Testament aren't the Gospels, but the letters of **Paul**. These were written to address specific issues in early church communities.

* **Galatians** (c. 48–50 AD) – Often considered the earliest, though some debate its placement.
* **1 & 2 Thessalonians** (c. 51 AD)
* **1 & 2 Corinthians** (c. 53–57 AD)
* **Romans** (c. 57 AD)
* **James** (Late 40s or 50s AD) – While not by Paul, many scholars place this very early.

## 2. The First Gospel & The Prison Epistles (c. 60 – 70 AD)

As the original eyewitnesses began to age, the need for written accounts of Jesus' life grew.

* **Mark** (c. 65–70 AD) – Widely considered the first Gospel written.
* **Philippians, Colossians, & Philemon** (c. 60–62 AD) – Written during Paul’s imprisonment.
* **Ephesians** (c. 60–62 AD)

## 3. The Synoptic Gospels & Later Paul (c. 70 – 85 AD)

Following the destruction of the Temple in Jerusalem (70 AD), we see the remaining biographical and instructional works emerge.

* **Matthew** (c. 70–85 AD)
* **Luke** (c. 80–85 AD)
* **Acts of the Apostles** (c. 80–85 AD) – Written by Luke as a "Volume II."
* **1 & 2 Timothy & Titus** (c. 63–67 AD or later) – The "Pastoral Epistles."
* **1 & 2 Peter** (c. 64–67 AD)
* **Hebrews** (c. 60–70 AD)

## 4. The Johannine Works & General Epistles (c. 85 – 95 AD)

The final era of New Testament writing is dominated by the "Johannine" school (John) and the final circular letters.

* **Gospel of John** (c. 90 AD)
* **1, 2, & 3 John** (c. 90–95 AD)
* **Jude** (c. 65–80 AD)
* **Revelation** (c. 95 AD) – Traditionally written during the reign of Domitian.

GEMNI AI Generated List.

### **@garlongreen2981** | 👍 4 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kuMhSFXnr5I&lc=Ugyr3lLX5LJ63bNDpQ14AaABAg)

This argument that Paul “hijacked” Christianity falls apart when you actually read Scripture and early church history.

Paul didn’t invent a new religion, Jesus personally chose him to spread the same gospel to the Gentiles (Acts 9:15). Paul then met with the original apostles, and they confirmed his message matched theirs (Gal. 2:6–9).

He constantly pointed people back to Jesus, not himself:
 “I resolved to know nothing… except Jesus Christ and him crucified.” (1 Cor. 2:2)
 “There is one God and one Mediator… the man Christ Jesus.” (1 Tim. 2:5)

Even Peter, Jesus’ closest disciple, said Paul’s letters were Scripture (2 Peter 3:15–16). That alone shuts down the myth that Paul distorted the gospel.

If Paul never existed, the gospel of grace, the resurrection, and salvation in Christ still stand straight from Jesus himself (John 1:17, Luke 24:46–47).

It’s a common argument that Christianity “shifted” from being about God to being about Jesus but the truth is, that shift wasn’t a distortion, it was the fulfillment of God’s own plan.

Jesus didn’t replace God. Jesus is God in the flesh.

“The Word became human and made his home among us… full of unfailing love and faithfulness.” (John 1:14)
“Anyone who has seen me has seen the Father.” (John 14:9)

The apostles didn’t invent that message. Peter who walked with Jesus personally was the first to declare:

“You are the Christ, the Son of the Living God.” (Matthew 16:16)

Even Paul, often criticized today, agreed with Jesus and Peter, and Peter affirmed Paul’s teachings as Scripture:

“…our dear brother Paul… wrote to you with the wisdom God gave him… some things hard to understand, which ignorant people distort.” (2 Peter 3:15–16)

So this isn’t a case of “Paul hijacked Christianity”  it’s that Paul explained what Jesus accomplished, just as Peter did.

Christianity didn’t stop being about God.
It revealed who God truly is not distant, but stepping into history through Christ to save us.

If Jesus really rose from the dead (which both Peter and Paul were willing to die proclaiming), then the only distortion is rejecting His identity.

The truth is: Christianity didn’t shift from God to Paul. It flowed through Paul because Jesus made it so.

### **@joycilenealves99**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kuMhSFXnr5I&lc=Ugw4e-ES7cBdT6Tb46h4AaABAg)

Revisão: O Jesus Gnóstico vs. O Jesus Bíblico

[00:00] - Introdução e Revisão: O professor revisa a aula anterior, contrastando o "Jesus Gnóstico" (que ensinava sobre a centelha divina interior e a rejeição da riqueza) com o "Jesus da Bíblia" (o filho de Deus que se sacrifica para redimir pecados).

[05:48] - O Caminho para Jerusalém: Explicação de como Jesus, ao sair das províncias e ir para Jerusalém, entrou em conflito com a elite religiosa, o que levou à sua crucificação.

[07:52] - Paralelos Literários: O professor aponta como a história de Jesus no Novo Testamento foi construída usando arquétipos de Sócrates (o portador da verdade perseguido) e Júlio César (o líder traído por seus seguidores).

Paulo de Tarso: O Verdadeiro Fundador

[09:41] - Quem foi Paulo: Descrição de Paulo como um "Judeu Helenizado", cidadão romano educado em retórica grega e filosofia. O professor afirma que o Cristianismo é uma religião sobre Jesus, criada por Paulo, e não a religião de Jesus [10:13].

[12:21] - Tiago, o Justo e os Ebionitas: Após a morte de Jesus, seu irmão Tiago lidera o movimento em Jerusalém (os "pobres"), mantendo as tradições judaicas. Isso sugere que os judeus não mataram Jesus, caso contrário, seus seguidores não estariam protegidos em Jerusalém [11:54].

[14:51] - O Momento de Damasco: A famosa conversão de Paulo. O professor nota como é estranho que Paulo se torne um seguidor fanático sem nunca ter conhecido Jesus pessoalmente.

As Mudanças Radicais de Paulo

[16:14] - Fé vs. Obras: Paulo altera o núcleo da mensagem: a salvação não vem mais pelas "boas obras" (como ensinava Jesus), mas exclusivamente pela "fé" em Jesus como salvador [16:31].

[16:53] - Organização e Hierarquia: Paulo introduz a estrutura de igreja e hierarquia, focando na conversão em massa e na expansão pelo Império Romano.

[17:41] - A Questão da Circuncisão: Para atrair gentios (não-judeus), Paulo decide que a circuncisão e as leis de Moisés não são mais necessárias, o que causa um conflito amargo com Tiago e os líderes em Jerusalém [18:33].

Paulo e o Império Romano (A Teoria do Espião)

[20:43] - O Confronto em Jerusalém e a Proteção Romana: Paulo é salvo de uma turba judaica por soldados romanos. Ele usa sua cidadania romana para apelar diretamente ao Imperador [22:04].

[23:00] - Análise de Atos dos Apóstolos: O professor lê trechos de Atos, destacando como Paulo vivia com privilégios em Roma, sugerindo que ele tinha um status especial ou proteção [27:34].

[37:10] - A Teoria do Espião/Colaborador: O professor propõe uma tese (para ser tomada com cautela) de que Paulo agia como um agente romano para neutralizar o fanatismo judaico. Ao espiritualizar o Messias e remover a necessidade de leis puristas (como a circuncisão), Paulo tornava os judeus mais fáceis de assimilar ao Império [43:25].

Legado e Revolução no Pensamento

[49:15] - Mudança no Conceito de Fé: Com Paulo, a fé deixa de ser uma experiência vivida para se tornar algo em que se deve acreditar dogmaticamente. Ele introduz o uso de "Milagres" para explicar as contradições bíblicas [49:53].

[52:18] - Autoria de Atos: Discussão sobre quem escreveu Atos dos Apóstolos (atribuído ao autor de Lucas) e como o livro funciona como uma "apologia" ou "limpeza de imagem" para Paulo [53:36].

[54:56] - Conclusão: Introdução para a próxima aula sobre monoteísmo como uma revolução na concepção da realidade.

​Jesus's initial instruction to His disciples, "Go nowhere among the Gentiles," was a temporary, strategic, and covenantal move, not a permanent exclusion. His ministry had to first prioritize the "lost sheep of the house of Israel" (Matthew 15:24) to fulfill Old Testament prophecies, establishing God's salvation through the Jewish people first. However, Jesus foreshadowed the broader mission by healing Gentiles and ultimately commanded the Great Commission—"make disciples of all nations" (Matthew 28:19)—after His resurrection, ensuring the Gospel's eventual global spread.

Before the law was given to Israel, FAITH was what brought a man or woman into relationship with God. Before Abraham was given the instruction to circumcise, the people of Yahweh (God of Israel) were not circumcising. 

So if those things were unnecessary (law and circumcision) to be saved, and by saved, I mean that one has a relationship with Yahweh that saves him, then why do we fault Paul for teaching this? 

There are numerous covenants in the bible and onle a select few are called "HOLY covenants". Isael's covenant is NOT one of those covenants that is called "Holy", but Abraham's covenant is, Noah's covenant was and the New covenant IS. The bible specifically calls these covenants the "Holy covenants". 

What makes a covenant a HOLY covenant is that it does not depend on flawed humans for the covenant to be upheld. Its more of a promise, because it is given by God/Yahweh to the people with no contingencies upon their actions to make the covenant stand. 

In the case of Noah, God promised that he would never gain flood the earth and this covenant was made not only to Noah, but also to the animals. Noah could do nothing to change it. 

The covenant given to Abraham was that his descendants would be as numerous as the stars and that the Messiah would descend from his bloodline to save the world. No action of Abraham's would affect this covenant. And the New covenant, which the book of Isaiah tells us Jesus himself....Jesus himself IS the New covenant and nothing man can do can change that. This is why these are HOLY covenants. 

When you study the bible enough to know WHY Abraham was given instructions to circumcise and why Israel was given the law then you understand why these things would come to an end in the AGE of the fish and that age began with Jesus. The age of the Ram/lamb that Moses ushered in would be contingent upon these old covenant laws. But the new age was ushered in by Jesus and he was preparing his people for the turn of the age from Ram/lamb to fish. 

Jesus's death as the Ram/lamb signified the END of the age of Ram/lamb. But there was this 40 year gap of time between the ages that two covenants and two ages were coexisting. This was from 30 AD-70 AD and the age of the Ram/lamb ended completely with the fall of the temple. These Jews had 40 years (a generation) to come out of the old age of the Ram/Lamb and embrace the new age of the fish.  Just like Moses people had 40 years in the wilderness to come out of the age of the OX and into the age of the Ram/Lamb. 

Just like Moses ground up the golden ox, threw it in the water and told them to drink of it, this signified the end of the age of the OX to enter into Ram/lamb. 

Jesus, who IS the Ram/lamb told his disciples to also drink of HIM (the lamb) and this signified the end of the age of Ram/Lamb. 

Paul understands these deeper things that 99.9% of Christians and of course non-Christians will never understand.

Paul was ministering to gentiles and Gentiles were not under the law. Paul was not preaching to "the house of Israel" like Jesus was. Paul was also preaching in a different time period. They were in the time of entering the new age of the fish. This would mean a new way of relating to God-not through rituals of the old covenant, but in spiritual applications of those old physical laws. 

Paul is a genius when it comes to scripture. Because of his partly secular background as a Roman, he had unique insight that traditional Jews did not and most of the apostles were traditional Jews. Just like the Hebrews were trying desperately to hold onto the old age of the OX and built the golden calf, the Jews of Jesus's time were trying desperately to hold onto the age of the Ram/lamb even though Jesus was in the process of bringing them into the age of the fish. 

This professor doesn't understand this. He is teaching from the viewpoint of tradition and not from what the bible actually teaches, but then again, most pastors are also guilty of this.

> **@ronb9515**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kuMhSFXnr5I&lc=UgxesKuD7pgVOVfoRHt4AaABAg.AOXakw-iANXAOYJOWYiV7K)  
>
> ​@M_S_G001Let's be clear on what the traditional view of the Christian church is on the authorship of the Gospel of Luke and the Acts of the Apostles. The traditional view is that the physician and companion of Paul, Luke was the author of these texts. Some scholars debate this authorship because of the so called lack of stated authorship by the author himself and the lack of references by early church fathers until the 180's CE. These scholars claim that no stated references close to the time of the original writing of the Gospel of Luke exist. Therefore, since almost 90 years between its writing and commentary by the early church fathers exists, this takes away from the credibility of authorship by Luke. However, it is largely held by the church at large and most scholars that Luke did in fact write these texts. Although some scholars have called into question the authorship of the Gospel of Luke and Acts, there is not overwhelming evidence that refutes the traditional church view. And, there is just as weighty an argument for the authenticity of Luke's  authorship above and beyond all of the other 3 Gospels. So, no, it is not widely held that the authorship of the Gospel of Luke and the Acts of the Apostles were not written by Luke, himself. The widely supported view is that Luke wrote both texts.

### **@immortalfool7627** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kuMhSFXnr5I&lc=UgzRVAaRlk1QvbTpqyp4AaABAg)

I enjoyed your presentation but I believe you have stated some information with a personal bias of some kind. You said quite few things that are not correct. I’m going to let Chat GPT do my heavy lifting.

Jesus was not a Gnostic in the historical or theological sense.

Longer, careful answer (as a Bible scholar and historically informed perspective):

⸻

1. What “Gnosticism” actually is

Gnosticism was not a single belief system but a family of movements (mostly 2nd–3rd century AD) that taught:
	•	Salvation comes through secret knowledge (gnōsis)
	•	The material world is evil or a mistake
	•	The true, highest God did not create the physical world
	•	The physical world was created by a lesser being (the Demiurge)
	•	The body is a prison; escape from matter is salvation
	•	Jesus is often a purely spiritual revealer, not fully incarnate

These ideas post-date Jesus and develop after Christianity spreads.

⸻

2. Jesus’ teachings directly contradict core Gnostic ideas

A. Creation is good (anti-Gnostic)

Jesus affirms Genesis:

“From the beginning of creation, God made them male and female” (Mark 10:6)

Gnosticism: matter = bad
Jesus: creation = God’s good work

⸻

B. The Incarnation (anti-Gnostic)

Christian faith hinges on this:

“The Word became flesh” (John 1:14)

Gnostics often denied Jesus truly had a physical body (this is called Docetism).

Jesus:
	•	Eats food
	•	Bleeds
	•	Suffers
	•	Is physically resurrected

“Touch me and see; a spirit does not have flesh and bones as you see that I have” (Luke 24:39)

That verse is explicitly anti-Gnostic.

⸻

C. Salvation is not secret knowledge

Jesus teaches openly:

“I have spoken openly to the world… I said nothing in secret” (John 18:20)

Gnosticism: only the enlightened few are saved
Jesus:

“Whoever believes in him shall not perish” (John 3:16)

Open invitation, not elite enlightenment.

⸻

D. Resurrection of the body

Gnostics rejected bodily resurrection.

Jesus affirms it:

“The hour is coming when all who are in the graves will hear his voice” (John 5:28)

Paul later fights Gnostic-like thinking directly:

“If Christ has not been raised, your faith is futile” (1 Corinthians 15)

⸻

3. Why some people think Jesus was a Gnostic

A. He uses symbolic language

Jesus speaks in parables and metaphors.
That does not equal Gnosticism.

Jewish rabbis commonly taught this way.

⸻

B. The Gospel of Thomas

Often cited by skeptics.

Facts:
	•	Written mid-2nd century
	•	Not written by Thomas
	•	Reflects later Gnostic reinterpretation
	•	Contradicts earlier sources

The canonical gospels (Mark, Matthew, Luke, John):
	•	Written earlier
	•	Widely circulated
	•	Consistent on core claims

⸻

C. “Secret teachings to disciples”

Jesus does explain things privately—but:
	•	Not secret salvation knowledge
	•	Not anti-creation
	•	Not body-denying
	•	Still grounded in Jewish theology

⸻

4. Historically: which came first?
	•	Jesus & apostles → 1st century
	•	Gnostic systems → mid-2nd century onward

Gnosticism is better understood as a reaction to Christianity, not its source.

Early church fathers (who were much closer historically):
	•	Irenaeus
	•	Justin Martyr
	•	Tertullian

They unanimously say:

Gnostics are distorting Jesus, not preserving his true teaching.

⸻

5. Theologically: Jesus fits Second Temple Judaism, not Gnosticism

Jesus:
	•	Quotes the Hebrew Scriptures constantly
	•	Affirms the Law, Prophets, and Psalms
	•	Worships the God of Israel
	•	Prays to the Creator
	•	Anticipates bodily resurrection
	•	Speaks of sin, repentance, forgiveness

Gnosticism:
	•	Rejects Jewish Scripture
	•	Rewrites Genesis
	•	Denies bodily hope
	•	Redefines God

They are fundamentally incompatible.

⸻

Bottom line

Jesus was:
	•	A Jewish Messiah
	•	Rooted in Hebrew Scripture
	•	Pro-creation
	•	Pro-incarnation
	•	Pro-resurrection

Gnosticism is:
	•	Later
	•	Anti-creation
	•	Anti-body
	•	Elitist
	•	Philosophically Greek, not Jewish

> That’s a bold chain of claims—but there’s no real evidence given, just assertions piled on assertions.  
>   
> 1. Claim: John the Baptist was an Essene, and so was Jesus.  
>    • No evidence cited.  
>    • Notably, even scholars who explore these questions don’t simply assume that. Bart Ehrman, a well‑known historian of early Christianity, explains that the identification of the Dead Sea Scrolls with the Essenes is widely accepted, but he doesn’t conclude that John or Jesus were Essenes, and in fact argues against John being an Essene.  
>    • So the comment’s starting premise is far from established; it’s conjecture, not documented fact.  
>   
> 2. Claim: Essenes practice Gnosticism.  
>    • Again, no supporting evidence shown.  
>    • Gnosticism is a modern umbrella term for a variety of religious ideas that arose in the late first century AD, often emphasizing secret knowledge over established authority. That’s the scholarly description.   
>    • Even if some later groups or texts are labeled Gnostic, it does not mean the Essenes as understood from ancient sources necessarily practiced Gnosticism, nor that the two groups are interchangeable or identical. The comment presents this as fact without proof.  
>   
> 3. Claim: Jesus was some unified bringer of older wisdom, basically a whitewashed version of Egyptian myths.  
>    • That’s a broad interpretive leap with no references to ancient sources, texts, or scholarly research.  
>    • It portrays the orthodox gospel narrative as derivative or invented without showing how. No documentation or reason is given why the gospel accounts should be reduced to that.  
>   
> Your comment leans on sweeping assertions—Essenes, Gnosticism, Jesus as syncretic figure—without offering any evidence, citations, or reasoning to connect them. A strong rebuttal isn’t about disagreeing in general; it’s about pointing out that the foundations of the argument are unsubstantiated. Until evidence is provided, these statements are just unsupported opinions, not a challenge to the orthodox view.

### **@char191991** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kuMhSFXnr5I&lc=UgxzKwyc03AlH_tsVY54AaABAg)

10:09 "That's why we beleive that not Jesus but Paul is the real founder of Christianity". Even though is an interesting perspective the one who is shared within this video, he is lacking grounds when saying that. There are multiple reason, but I will share a few in this comment:

1) The first book of the New Testament of the Christian Bible is James, which was one of Jesus' brother who didn't beleive in Jesus when he was alive, written around year 44-62 after Christ (just 11 years after his death and resurrection). James is not Paul, and had a clear sense of Jesus as Lord and God, see verse 1: "James, a servant of God and of the Lord Jesus Christ, To the twelve tribes in the Dispersion: Greetings." (James 1:1 English Standard Version, ESV). So, James, not Paul, was the first human author writting about Jesus as Lord.

2) There is no Christian Faith without the Resurrection of Christ (1 Corinthians 15:1-10). If this story about Jesus Christ was a make up history from Paul, why it wasn't debunked by the people of his time? Also, why Paul would write about this since he was in the duty of persecute and throw Christian into jail and trial within the Jew Sanhedrin (see Acts 9). The apostle Luke gives a clue when writting to Theophilus in the beginning of the book of Acts: "In the first book, O Theophilus, I have dealt with all that Jesus began to do and teach, until the day when he was taken up, after he had given commands through the Holy Spirit to the apostles whom he had chosen. He presented himself alive to them after his suffering by many proofs, appearing to them during forty days and speaking about the kingdom of God." (Acts 1:1-3, ESV). So, the facts of the death and resurrection of Jesus Christ were so clear that nobody argued against them.

3) The great questions here are two:
   - 1. How all the followers of Christ, who were afraid and ran from Jerusalem after the death of their leader, were then willing to give their lives for the faith that Jesus Christ proclaimed? 
   - 2. Why the Christian Bible, in particular the New Testament, have more ancient copies of its originals compared to all the other ancient literature? Scholars have found more than 5,000 copies of manuscripts of the New Testament, generated by an active comunity of believers in the first century. Again, as early as 11 years after the death and resurrection of Christ.

4) During the time when Paul wrote his epistles to the church, in particular Galacia's church (first epistle written by Paul, around the year 50 after Christ), other human authors were writting to the Church as well. One of them was Matthew, a Jewish disciple of Christ, who was also a tax collector before joining Him. Matthew wrote his Gospel around years 50-61 after Christ (so around just 20-30 years after the resurrection), and he wrote the following about Him:

"Then Jesus came from Galilee to the Jordan to John, to be baptized by him. John would have prevented him, saying, “I need to be baptized by you, and do you come to me?” But Jesus answered him, “Let it be so now, for thus it is fitting for us to fulfill all righteousness.” Then he consented. And when Jesus was baptized, immediately he went up from the water, and behold, the heavens were opened to him, and he saw the Spirit of God descending like a dove and coming to rest on him; and behold, a voice from heaven said, “This is my beloved Son, with whom I am well pleased.” (Matthew 3:13-17, ESV). 

This clearly shows that in Matthew's perspective (not Paul's), Jesus was the Son of God, title that only belongs to God himself according to previous Jew tradition. In fact, quoting prophet Isaiah in chapter 1:23b, he says that Jesus was "Immanuel (which means, God with us)". What I want to point out here with this is that not Paul, but others that knew Jesus before, were also indicating that Jesus was 1) Lord, 2) Son of God and 3) Immanuel (which means, God with us). 

5) A next argument is that, following that Matthew was a direct eyewitness of the teachings of Jesus Christ and wrote the things about Jesus mentioned in 4), that shows that the vision of Christ as Lord, Son of God, and God with us (which is the same as saying God the Son), is not unique of Paul and was a vision though by Jesus himself (in fact, it was the reason why the religious leaders of that time killed him, because they though it was heresy). So, being that the case, the Pauline letters are not a made up story done by the roman empire, but a clear and trustwhorty account of the facts that happend when Jesus Christ came to the earth and its implications to the ones believving in Him.    

There are much more arguments that could be posted to rebuke this affirmation, but I'm glad to came up to this video, because it shows me how Christianity is perceived by other cultures, and by people who do not beleive in Jesus as the Lord. This is interesting. It is good to know that these topics can be studied and discussed academically.

I will leave this post here, so maybe it could help anyone listening into this lecture. Thank you for sharing it! Have a nice day.

I believe he has some keen insight into Paul's teachings and then jumps to some way off conclusions. I have been reading the 2000-page Urantia Book since 1972. It says many of the same things. It says that Christianity is a religion about Jesus and not the religion of Jesus. It says that Paul got the idea of atonement and original sin all by himself -- that idea never came from Jesus. It says that Christianity incorporated many pagan beliefs and rituals, early on, to draw more people to the faith, and it incorporated Greek philosophy and so it became very Westernized, whereas the religion of Jesus was for, ad appealed to, all people, East and West. 

But the great Urantia Book also says that Paul, notwithstanding his flaws, was the greatest teacher our planet has ever seen. This makes sense, since he was so largely responsible for spreading Christianity far and wide. He certainly was not a Roman spy. He did meet Jesus on his way to Damascus, he was completely converted, and he did spend the rest of his life teaching a religion about Jesus, and being persecuted for it, jailed, and almost killed many times. (None of that would have happened if he were a spy.) He was a great man -- he  just got a few things very wrong, and those few things changed the religion of Jesus, fundamentally. Jesus never advocated poverty; in fact, many of his disciples were wealthy. How could the Ebionites be the bearers of the pure doctrine of Christ, if the oldest reference to the Ebionite sect is in Irenaeus of Lyons' "Against Heresies" (book 1.26), a text dating from around 180 AD? There's no mention of them before Irenaeus, which already makes appealing to them problematic, as there's no apparent reason why a group from the late 2nd century should be considered as tracing back to the preaching of Christ and His Apostles. I don't know what references he consulted about Paul, but he managed to get one hundred percent of his observations wrong. Paul never converted to Judaism; he was Jewish by birth, raised under Gamaliel, and became a doctor of the law. In other words, he had only something to lose by becoming a Christian. He wasn't rich; he survived by making tents. Regarding the doctrinal differences with the other apostles, this matter was resolved when, in a meeting with Paul, they agreed with him and decided that Gentiles should not keep the Law, except by abstaining from idolatry, prostitution, and the consumption of blood. To insinuate that he was a secret agent of Rome was the height of theological ignorance, because, being a Roman citizen and with no evidence of civil crime against him, he had the right to rent his own house under military surveillance while awaiting Caesar's judgment. Proof of this is that later, under Emperor Nero, he was imprisoned in harsh conditions (a dungeon) before his execution. Did Rome kill its agent? In his conversation with Nicodemus (who was already performing good works), Christ preached that there is only one way to be saved: to be born again. All the apostles preached that salvation comes through believing in Christ and not only through good works, as the Jews and other religious people already practiced. But at the same time his Jewishness would be a nagging reality. Being educated in the Greek tradition, he was perhaps even abused as a boy which traumatized him. Also his Roman culture involved polytheist idol worship and sexual decadence, a stark contrast to Judaism. An angry and confused Paul pivots to the extreme and becomes a Pharisee (a rather fascist bunch.) He takes out his anger and frustration at life on Christians by killing and torturing them. But all this killing and torturing starts to get to him. Maybe even makes him a little crazy. Then suddenly he sees a way out through this pacifist religion where all sins are forgiven and your past becomes irrelevant. Also as a highly educated wealthy man with powerful contacts, he easily finds a leadership role in this new religion, fulfilling the ambitions that come from being raised in privilege and giving him personal meaning. Paul found contentment in Christianity.

The earliest Christian writings outside the New Testament  
> These are the texts closest in time to the apostles — roughly 70–150 AD. They’re not fringe; they’re the mainstream voices of the early churches.  
>   
> 1 Clement (c. 96 AD)  
> Calls Jesus the “Scepter of the Majesty of God.”  
>   
> Speaks of Christ as pre‑existent and active in creation.  
>   
> This is a Roman church document written while some apostles were still alive.  
>   
> Ignatius of Antioch (c. 110 AD) — the strongest early witness  
> Ignatius repeatedly calls Jesus:  
>   
> “Our God, Jesus Christ”  
>   
> “God come in the flesh”  
>   
> “God’s blood”  
>   
> Ignatius is a bishop writing on his way to martyrdom. His letters are some of the clearest early affirmations of Jesus’ divinity.  
>   
> The Didache (c. 70–120 AD)  
> Doesn’t explicitly call Jesus “God,” but:  
>   
> Treats Jesus as the divine Lord of the church.  
>   
> Uses Trinitarian baptismal formula identical to Matthew 28.  
>   
> It assumes a divine Christ without arguing for it.  
>   
> The Epistle of Barnabas (c. 100 AD)  
> Interprets Old Testament passages as spoken by Jesus before His incarnation — implying pre‑existence and divine authority.  
>   
> The Shepherd of Hermas (c. 100–140 AD)  
> This one is interesting:  
>   
> It presents a high Christology (pre‑existence, divine mission).  
>   
> But it’s less explicit than Ignatius.  
>   
> It shows how early Christians were still working out categories.  
>   
> Still, Jesus is treated as more than a mere human.

I think Jesus believed the creator of this world is good but that we were just living incorrectly. I think he thought this realm was real (not fake) but that the spirit is everlasting even though the body isn't. And when Jesus wanted people to get rid of their things, I don't think it's because Jesus disliked things. I think it's because 1) giving your things to others is a form of love. If you want people to love you, you must be willing to love them first, 2) you don't really have a need for things if you're on a mission to save humanity from destruction. You are focused on saving the world. 3) things aren't important but making sure others are ok is important. And by giving away your things you are naturally exalted by others. In that way, Jesus was hoping to become the new king of Earth by people raising him up because of his good works. I don't think he expected to be crucified. He was hoping to become the king of Earth (Messiah).

Then I think Paul made up the resurection and miracles to try and validate him as the Messiah since the Jews wouldn't believe in him unless "he was alive" somehow and the miracles were extra since people are more willing to believe people who do miracles. Because according to Jews the Messiah was living, so Paul probably said, "well he resurected so there you go he is technically still alive."

Because the fact of the matter is that historically people are spiritually weak. They have a tendency to obey strong men. People that can perform miracles and who you need to be afraid of. They are easily tempted. They are insecure and need validation. So convincing people love is the way isn't as easy as waving miracles in front of their face.

> @alexander_sinclair Again, The passage with the rich young ruler isn’t a universal rule for all believers to sell everything. In the text, Jesus is responding to one man’s specific issue. The language is second-person singular (he says  thou, not ye), and Mark notes that Jesus “looked at him and loved him,” which signals a personal diagnosis, not a general command. The man’s reaction—he walks away sad because he had great wealth—shows the instruction targeted his particular attachment. Jesus later turns to the disciples and gives the general teaching: wealth makes entering the kingdom difficult. So the universal point is detachment from whatever controls you, not mandatory total poverty for every disciple.

### **@truthandlifeapochiministry** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kuMhSFXnr5I&lc=UgwCeN6x9u61hvmri454AaABAg)

The New Testament does not show a conflict between Jesus and Paul when their teachings are read in context. Jesus preached the coming Kingdom of God, calling people to repent, believe, and follow Him (Mark 1:14–15). Paul preached that salvation comes through faith in Christ’s death and resurrection (1 Corinthians 15:1–4). Jesus upheld the Law’s purpose and revealed its fullness (Matthew 5:17), while Paul taught that believers are justified by faith apart from the works of the Law (Romans 3:28). Both taught love as the foundation of God’s will (John 13:34; Romans 13:8–10). Their messages are complementary, not contradictory.
